People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I suppose I have, though I don’t like to make it sound too dramatic. I used to really look forward to things—planning my garden beds, quilting with the ladies, going to theater outings, even fussing and arguing at book club in a friendly way.

Now I see those things on the calendar and I just feel… almost nothing. Like they belong to someone I used to be. I still try sometimes, because I know I should, but it’s not the same. Is that the sort of thing you mean?

Unless I’m misunderstanding what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself jumping or startling more easily than you used to, even when there's no real danger?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve really noticed that, no. I mean, I might get a little surprised if something is very loud, but I think most people would. I wouldn’t want to overstate it. It’s not something I’ve been especially aware of.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel numb or detached from the world around you?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, yes. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but… even when I’m sitting with people I’ve known for years at the senior center, I can feel sort of separate from them. Like they’re all talking and laughing on one side of a window, and I’m on the other side. I’m there, but not quite… with them. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself thinking that you're to blame for your friend's death or that you could have done something to prevent it?\n\nPatient: I do, yes… though I know it probably isn’t fair to say it that way. I keep going over it in my head, thinking if I’d picked a different route, or if I’d pushed harder about that crossing, maybe she’d still be here. And then I get angry all over again—at the driver, and at the town for letting that crossing be so unsafe. I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these feelings of guilt, fear, and anger since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: It’s been… well, since it happened, really. Since my friend was killed on that walking tour. That was about two years and three months ago now. I suppose I keep hoping it should have eased more by now, but those feelings have been there from then on.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided other activities or places besides the intersection and the tours or safety meetings since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Well… mostly it’s those things, I think. I do go out of my way not to be near that intersection, even if it means taking a longer way around. And I’ve stopped signing up for most of the walking tours, and even some of the safety meetings, because the conversation always seems to come back around to what happened there, and I just… I can’t sit through it very well.\n\nI don’t know if that counts as “other” places or not, but that’s been the main way I’ve avoided things.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy before your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have, though I don’t like to make it sound too dramatic. I used to really look forward to things—planning my garden beds, quilting with the ladies, going to theater outings, even fussing and arguing at book club in a friendly way.\n\nNow I see those things on the calendar and I just feel… almost nothing. Like they belong to someone I used to be. I still try sometimes, because I know I should, but it’s not the same. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided forming close relationships with others since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’ve avoided people exactly… I mean, I still go to the senior center and sit with folks I’ve known for years. I try to be pleasant.\n\nBut… yes, I suppose I don’t feel close in the same way. Even when they’re all chatting around me, it can feel like they’re on one side of a window and I’m on the other. I’m there, but not really… with them. Does that make sense?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   {

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF43.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose I’m here because I haven’t really been myself. Since my friend was killed, I’ve had this… constant mix of guilt and fear and anger that just doesn’t seem to let up. I kept thinking it would ease with time, like people said it would, and I don’t want to make too much of it, but it really hasn’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people, places, or activities since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, a bit. I try not to make a fuss about it, but I do go out of my way not to pass that intersection. And I’ve stopped signing up for most of the tours or the safety meetings, because somehow the talk always seems to come back around to what happened there, and I just… I can’t sit through it. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're reliving the moment your friend was killed?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, not exactly like I’m there again all the time, but sometimes it comes back much too clearly. A crosswalk signal will change, or I’ll hear a truck growling by, or even just see one of those walking-tour maps, and suddenly the whole thing is in my head. I can see my friend stepping off the curb before I can stop it. It’s… very sharp. I don’t know if that’s what you mean by reliving it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're outside of your body or watching yourself from somewhere else when these memories come back?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re describing, at least. I feel very frightened and caught up in the memory, but I wouldn’t say I’m watching myself from somewhere else. Unless I’m misunderstanding what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself jumping or startling more easily than you used to, even when there's no real danger?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve really noticed that, no. I mean, I might get a little surprised if something is very loud, but I think most people would. I wouldn’t want to overstate it. It’s not something I’ve been especially aware of.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel numb or detached from the world around you?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, yes. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but… even when I’m sitting with people I’ve known for years at the senior center, I can feel sort of separate from them. Like they’re all talking and laughing on one side of a window, and I’m on the other side. I’m there, but not quite… with them. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself thinking that you're to blame for your friend's death or that you could have done something to prevent it?\n\nPatient: I do, yes… though I know it probably isn’t fair to say it that way. I keep going over it in my head, thinking if I’d picked a different route, or if I’d pushed harder about that crossing, maybe she’d still be here. And then I get angry all over again—at the driver, and at the town for letting that crossing be so unsafe. I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these feelings of guilt, fear, and anger since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: It’s been… well, since it happened, really. Since my friend was killed on that walking tour. That was about two years and three months ago now. I suppose I keep hoping it should have eased more by now, but those feelings have been there from then on.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided other activities or places besides the intersection and the tours or safety meetings since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Well… mostly it’s those things, I think. I do go out of my way not to be near that intersection, even if it means taking a longer way around. And I’ve stopped signing up for most of the walking tours, and even some of the safety meetings, because the conversation always seems to come back around to what happened there, and I just… I can’t sit through it very well.\n\nI don’t know if that counts as “other” places or not, but that’s been the main way I’ve avoided things.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy before your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have, though I don’t like to make it sound too dramatic. I used to really look forward to things—planning my garden beds, quilting with the ladies, going to theater outings, even fussing and arguing at book club in a friendly way.\n\nNow I see those things on the calendar and I just feel… almost nothing. Like they belong to someone I used to be. I still try sometimes, because I know I should, but it’s not the same. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided forming close relationships with others since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’ve avoided people exactly… I mean, I still go to the senior center and sit with folks I’ve known for years. I try to be pleasant.\n\nBut… yes, I suppose I don’t feel close in the same way. Even when they’re all chatting around me, it can feel like they’re on one side of a window and I’m on the other. I’m there, but not really… with them. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you stopped doing anything else that you used to enjoy because it reminds you of your friend or the accident?\n\nPatient: Mostly the same things I mentioned, I think… I don’t want to overstate it. I still do some things.\n\nBut I do go out of my way not to pass that intersection, even if it makes the trip longer. And I’ve stopped signing up for most of the tours and those safety meetings, because somehow the talk always seems to come back around to what happened there, and I just… I can’t bear sitting through that. Is that what you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I don’t know if that’s what you mean by reliving it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're outside of your body or watching yourself from somewhere else when these memories come back?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re describing, at least. I feel very frightened and caught up in the memory, but I wouldn’t say I’m watching myself from somewhere else. Unless I’m misunderstanding what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself jumping or startling more easily than you used to, even when there's no real danger?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve really noticed that, no. I mean, I might get a little surprised if something is very loud, but I think most people would. I wouldn’t want to overstate it. It’s not something I’ve been especially aware of.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel numb or detached from the world around you?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, yes. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but… even when I’m sitting with people I’ve known for years at the senior center, I can feel sort of separate from them. Like they’re all talking and laughing on one side of a window, and I’m on the other side. I’m there, but not quite… with them. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself thinking that you're to blame for your friend's death or that you could have done something to prevent it?\n\nPatient: I do, yes… though I know it probably isn’t fair to say it that way. I keep going over it in my head, thinking if I’d picked a different route, or if I’d pushed harder about that crossing, maybe she’d still be here. And then I get angry all over again—at the driver, and at the town for letting that crossing be so unsafe. I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these feelings of guilt, fear, and anger since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: It’s been… well, since it happened, really. Since my friend was killed on that walking tour. That was about two years and three months ago now. I suppose I keep hoping it should have eased more by now, but those feelings have been there from then on.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided other activities or places besides the intersection and the tours or safety meetings since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Well… mostly it’s those things, I think. I do go out of my way not to be near that intersection, even if it means taking a longer way around. And I’ve stopped signing up for most of the walking tours, and even some of the safety meetings, because the conversation always seems to come back around to what happened there, and I just… I can’t sit through it very well.\n\nI don’t know if that counts as “other” places or not, but that’s been the main way I’ve avoided things.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy before your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have, though I don’t like to make it sound too dramatic. I used to really look forward to things—planning my garden beds, quilting with the ladies, going to theater outings, even fussing and arguing at book club in a friendly way.\n\nNow I see those things on the calendar and I just feel… almost nothing. Like they belong to someone I used to be. I still try sometimes, because I know I should, but it’s not the same. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided forming close relationships with others since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’ve avoided people exactly… I mean, I still go to the senior center and sit with folks I’ve known for years. I try to be pleasant.\n\nBut… yes, I suppose I don’t feel close in the same way. Even when they’re all chatting around me, it can feel like they’re on one side of a window and I’m on the other. I’m there, but not really… with them. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you stopped doing anything else that you used to enjoy because it reminds you of your friend or the accident?\n\nPatient: Mostly the same things I mentioned, I think… I don’t want to overstate it. I still do some things.\n\nBut I do go out of my way not to pass that intersection, even if it makes the trip longer. And I’ve stopped signing up for most of the tours and those safety meetings, because somehow the talk always seems to come back around to what happened there, and I just… I can’t bear sitting through that. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you significantly changed your daily routine to avoid reminders of your friend's death?\n\nPatient: I suppose I have, yes… though I try not to make a big production of it.\n\nI take different routes now so I don’t have to go through that intersection, even if it adds time. And I’ve mostly stopped putting my name down for the tours or those safety meetings, because the conversation somehow always drifts back to what happened, and I just… I can’t sit there and listen to it. Is that what you mean by changing my routine?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Since my friend was killed on that walking tour. That was about two years and three months ago now. I suppose I keep hoping it should have eased more by now, but those feelings have been there from then on.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided other activities or places besides the intersection and the tours or safety meetings since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Well… mostly it’s those things, I think. I do go out of my way not to be near that intersection, even if it means taking a longer way around. And I’ve stopped signing up for most of the walking tours, and even some of the safety meetings, because the conversation always seems to come back around to what happened there, and I just… I can’t sit through it very well.\n\nI don’t know if that counts as “other” places or not, but that’s been the main way I’ve avoided things.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy before your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have, though I don’t like to make it sound too dramatic. I used to really look forward to things—planning my garden beds, quilting with the ladies, going to theater outings, even fussing and arguing at book club in a friendly way.\n\nNow I see those things on the calendar and I just feel… almost nothing. Like they belong to someone I used to be. I still try sometimes, because I know I should, but it’s not the same. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided forming close relationships with others since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’ve avoided people exactly… I mean, I still go to the senior center and sit with folks I’ve known for years. I try to be pleasant.\n\nBut… yes, I suppose I don’t feel close in the same way. Even when they’re all chatting around me, it can feel like they’re on one side of a window and I’m on the other. I’m there, but not really… with them. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you stopped doing anything else that you used to enjoy because it reminds you of your friend or the accident?\n\nPatient: Mostly the same things I mentioned, I think… I don’t want to overstate it. I still do some things.\n\nBut I do go out of my way not to pass that intersection, even if it makes the trip longer. And I’ve stopped signing up for most of the tours and those safety meetings, because somehow the talk always seems to come back around to what happened there, and I just… I can’t bear sitting through that. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you significantly changed your daily routine to avoid reminders of your friend's death?\n\nPatient: I suppose I have, yes… though I try not to make a big production of it.\n\nI take different routes now so I don’t have to go through that intersection, even if it adds time. And I’ve mostly stopped putting my name down for the tours or those safety meetings, because the conversation somehow always drifts back to what happened, and I just… I can’t sit there and listen to it. Is that what you mean by changing my routine?\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided many social activities because they remind you of your friend or the accident?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t mean that I’ve become a hermit or anything, I still try to be social when I can.\n\nBut I do avoid things where I know it might come up. I go out of my way not to pass that intersection, and I’ve stopped signing up for most of the tours or those safety meetings, because somehow people start talking about what happened there, and I just… I can’t bear it. I hope that answers what you’re asking.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. And I’ve stopped signing up for most of the walking tours, and even some of the safety meetings, because the conversation always seems to come back around to what happened there, and I just… I can’t sit through it very well.\n\nI don’t know if that counts as “other” places or not, but that’s been the main way I’ve avoided things.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy before your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have, though I don’t like to make it sound too dramatic. I used to really look forward to things—planning my garden beds, quilting with the ladies, going to theater outings, even fussing and arguing at book club in a friendly way.\n\nNow I see those things on the calendar and I just feel… almost nothing. Like they belong to someone I used to be. I still try sometimes, because I know I should, but it’s not the same. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided forming close relationships with others since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’ve avoided people exactly… I mean, I still go to the senior center and sit with folks I’ve known for years. I try to be pleasant.\n\nBut… yes, I suppose I don’t feel close in the same way. Even when they’re all chatting around me, it can feel like they’re on one side of a window and I’m on the other. I’m there, but not really… with them. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you stopped doing anything else that you used to enjoy because it reminds you of your friend or the accident?\n\nPatient: Mostly the same things I mentioned, I think… I don’t want to overstate it. I still do some things.\n\nBut I do go out of my way not to pass that intersection, even if it makes the trip longer. And I’ve stopped signing up for most of the tours and those safety meetings, because somehow the talk always seems to come back around to what happened there, and I just… I can’t bear sitting through that. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you significantly changed your daily routine to avoid reminders of your friend's death?\n\nPatient: I suppose I have, yes… though I try not to make a big production of it.\n\nI take different routes now so I don’t have to go through that intersection, even if it adds time. And I’ve mostly stopped putting my name down for the tours or those safety meetings, because the conversation somehow always drifts back to what happened, and I just… I can’t sit there and listen to it. Is that what you mean by changing my routine?\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided many social activities because they remind you of your friend or the accident?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t mean that I’ve become a hermit or anything, I still try to be social when I can.\n\nBut I do avoid things where I know it might come up. I go out of my way not to pass that intersection, and I’ve stopped signing up for most of the tours or those safety meetings, because somehow people start talking about what happened there, and I just… I can’t bear it. I hope that answers what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that avoiding these reminders of your friend's death has significantly impacted your ability to participate in daily life or maintain relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has, more than I like to admit.\n\nI used to help organize programs at the senior center, and I managed some of the volunteer budgets, and I’ve had to step back from that because I just can’t keep up reliably the way I should. I don’t want to let people down.\n\nAnd I’ve pulled away from some of the clubs and activities that used to sort of structure my week. I still see people, but not like before. So… yes, I suppose it has affected daily life and relationships some. Does that make sense?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose I’m here because I haven’t really been myself. Since my friend was killed, I’ve had this… constant mix of guilt and fear and anger that just doesn’t seem to let up. I keep going over it in my head, thinking if I’d picked a different route, or if I’d pushed harder about that crossing, maybe she’d still be here. And then I get angry all over again—at the driver, and at the town for letting that crossing be so unsafe. I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these feelings of guilt, fear, and anger since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: It’s been… well, since it happened, really. Since my friend was killed on that walking tour. That was about two years and three months ago now. I suppose I keep hoping it should have eased more by now, but those feelings have been there from then on.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided other activities or places besides the intersection and the tours or safety meetings since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Well… mostly it’s those things, I think. I do go out of my way not to be near that intersection, even if it means taking a longer way around. And I’ve stopped signing up for most of the walking tours, and even some of the safety meetings, because the conversation always seems to come back around to what happened there, and I just… I can’t sit through it very well.\n\nI don’t know if that counts as “other” places or not, but that’s been the main way I’ve avoided things.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy before your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have, though I don’t like to make it sound too dramatic. I used to really look forward to things—planning my garden beds, quilting with the ladies, going to theater outings, even fussing and arguing at book club in a friendly way.\n\nNow I see those things on the calendar and I just feel… almost nothing. Like they belong to someone I used to be. I still try sometimes, because I know I should, but it’s not the same. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided forming close relationships with others since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’ve avoided people exactly… I mean, I still go to the senior center and sit with folks I’ve known for years. I try to be pleasant.\n\nBut… yes, I suppose I don’t feel close in the same way. Even when they’re all chatting around me, it can feel like they’re on one side of a window and I’m on the other. I’m there, but not really… with them. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you stopped doing anything else that you used to enjoy because it reminds you of your friend or the accident?\n\nPatient: Mostly the same things I mentioned, I think… I don’t want to overstate it. I still do some things.\n\nBut I do go out of my way not to pass that intersection, even if it makes the trip longer. And I’ve stopped signing up for most of the tours and those safety meetings, because somehow the talk always seems to come back around to what happened there, and I just… I can’t bear sitting through that. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you significantly changed your daily routine to avoid reminders of your friend's death?\n\nPatient: I suppose I have, yes… though I try not to make a big production of it.\n\nI take different routes now so I don’t have to go through that intersection, even if it adds time. And I’ve mostly stopped putting my name down for the tours or those safety meetings, because the conversation somehow always drifts back to what happened, and I just… I can’t sit there and listen to it. Is that what you mean by changing my routine?\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided many social activities because they remind you of your friend or the accident?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t mean that I’ve become a hermit or anything, I still try to be social when I can.\n\nBut I do avoid things where I know it might come up. I go out of my way not to pass that intersection, and I’ve stopped signing up for most of the tours or those safety meetings, because somehow people start talking about what happened there, and I just… I can’t bear it. I hope that answers what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that avoiding these reminders of your friend's death has significantly impacted your ability to participate in daily life or maintain relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has, more than I like to admit.\n\nI used to help organize programs at the senior center, and I managed some of the volunteer budgets, and I’ve had to step back from that because I just can’t keep up reliably the way I should. I don’t want to let people down.\n\nAnd I’ve pulled away from some of the clubs and activities that used to sort of structure my week. I still see people, but not like before. So… yes, I suppose it has affected daily life and relationships some. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you found it increasingly difficult to confront or think about the circumstances surrounding your friend's death over time?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it has gotten harder in a way. I don’t sit myself down and think, “I’m avoiding this,” but I do notice I’ll arrange things so I don’t have to face it.\n\nI still go out of my way not to go through that intersection, even when it’s inconvenient. And I’ve stopped signing up for most of the tours or safety meetings because the talk so often circles back to what happened there, and I just… I can’t bear sitting through it.\n\nI hope that’s the kind of thing you mean.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you significantly changed your daily routine to avoid reminders of your friend's death?\n\nPatient: I suppose I have, yes… though I try not to make a big production of it.\n\nI take different routes now so I don’t have to go through that intersection, even if it adds time. And I’ve mostly stopped putting my name down for the tours or those safety meetings, because the conversation somehow always drifts back to what happened, and I just… I can’t sit there and listen to it. Is that what you mean by changing my routine?\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided many social activities because they remind you of your friend or the accident?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I go out of my way not to pass that intersection, and I’ve stopped signing up for most of the tours or those safety meetings, because somehow people start talking about what happened there, and I just… I can’t bear it. I hope that answers what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that avoiding these reminders of your friend's death has significantly impacted your ability to participate in daily life or maintain relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has, more than I like to admit.\n\nI used to help organize programs at the senior center, and I managed some of the volunteer budgets, and I’ve had to step back from that because I just can’t keep up reliably the way I should. I’ll go out of my way not to pass that intersection, even if it’s not the most sensible route. And I’ve mostly stopped signing up for tours or safety meetings, because the conversations so often circle back to what happened there, and I just can’t sit with that.\n\nSo yes, I suppose it has become more frequent… maybe more automatic.
